# Fortress gap ledger

One row per claim. **status** — `POSITIVE-VERIFIED` (works; ran, output recorded) ·
`NEGATIVE-VERIFIED` (spec settles it, reproducer confirms) · `NEGATIVE-BOUNDED`
(failure reproduces; the mechanisms tried are named and are not exhaustive) ·
`CONTESTED` · `RETIRED` (a source claim falsified here; kept so it is not re-derived).
**class** — `implementation gap` · `library gap vs spec` · `library bug` ·
`design limit` · `deliberate` · `typesetter` · `packaging`.
Spec citations are to `Specification/**/*.tex` (line numbers as of this tree;
the chapters cited are byte-identical in `Specification-1.0-frozen/`).
Reproducer paths are relative to `explorations/`; `gNN` means `gap-ledger-probes/gNN.fss`.

## 1. Grammar and lexing

| # | claim | status | class | spec citation | reproducer | found by | notes / workaround |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `x[i][j]` is grammatical and left-associative, but the walk interpreter invokes the **first** subscript with an empty argument list (`Failed to find any matching overload, args = ()`) | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | `appendices/grammars/concrete-syntax.tex:937-953`; `basic/operators/precedence.tex:44-51`; `basic/operators/juxtameaning.tex:164-168` | `g01` | astra, blinded, review | Mechanism, new here: a chained subscript parses as `MathPrimary` (not `SubscriptExpr`); `Evaluator.java:1052` routes it to `evalAndInvokeMethod`, whose `evalInvocationArgs` (`Evaluator.java:1251-1252`) does `exprs.subList(1,…)` — correct for a juxtaposition list, fatal for a subscript list. Workaround `(x[i])[j]`. One-line fix. |
| 2 | the same defect **silently returns the wrong element** when the subscript has more than one index: `m[3,7]^2` evaluates `(m[7])^2` | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | as row 1 | `g02` | (this merge) | `m[3,7]=1037`, `m[7]=107`; `m[3,7]^2` prints `11449`, not `1075369`. No error, no warning. |
| 3 | `x[i]^2` (subscript then superscript) fails the same way | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | as row 1; `basic/operators/juxtameaning.tex:150-151` forbids only the reverse order (`x^2[i]`) | `g02` | astra, blinded, review | Workaround `(x[i])^2`. |
| 4 | `f()[i]` (call then subscript) is a **static error by specification**, not an implementation defect | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/operators/juxtameaning.tex:156-162` — "It is a static error if … the argument is immediately followed by a non-expression element" | `g03` | blinded | Interpreter reports that sentence verbatim. Workaround `(f())[i]`. |
| 5 | U+2211 `∑` lexes as a prefix operator, never as an accumulator; only ASCII `SUM` is a big operator | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | `basic/expressions/reductions.tex:20` (`Accumulator ::= Σ ∣ Π ∣ BIG (Encloser ∣ Op)`) | `sum-probes/p13_unicode_sigma.fss` | ours | `Operator prefix SUM is not defined.` Fortify renders ASCII `SUM` as `Σ`, so the *rendered* form is unaffected. |
| 6 | a reduction is a `FlowExpr`: it cannot be an operand of an infix operator without parentheses | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `appendices/grammars/concrete-syntax.tex:971-974` | `g07` | ours | `2.0 / SUM[u <- e] u` → `Syntax Error`; `2.0 / (SUM[u <- e] u)` is required. |
| 7 | an all-uppercase word with ≥2 distinct letters (`BOS`, `GPT`) is an **operator word**, not an identifier | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/lexical-structure.tex:1167-1172` | `fable-review-probes/RvwNameKeywords.fss` | blinded | `Operator prefix BOS is not defined.` Rename. Met again by the APL rungs on the sub-language's own names, where neither an `Id` gap nor a grammar alternative can carry such a word: `BLK ← 4 ⋄ BLK` is a `Syntax Error` where `Blk ← 4 ⋄ Blk` runs, and single capitals (`Q`, `K`) are fine (`apl/rung-5/v06_caps.fss` + `.out.0`/`.out`, re-run in the APL merge; apl gaps row 78, merged here). Row 193 is the same rule met on a *nonterminal's* name, and sharpens this row's “≥2 distinct letters”: that is the spec's rule, and the implementation's is looser. |
| 8 | `value`, `at`, `type`, `unit`, `of`, `most`, `label` … are reserved words | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit (the `label` diagnostic: implementation gap) | `basic/lexical-structure.tex:776-777` + `fortress/fortress-keywords.tex` (86 reserved words; `label` is at `:23`) | `g08`; `run-c/probes/g148_label.fss` | blinded, run-c | `value = 3` → `Syntax Error`. Run C's row 148 is merged here, so the ledger's numbering skips 148 and `run-c/gaps.md`'s row 148 resolves to this row: a *parameter* named `label` is rejected twice with `Missing label from a label expression.`, at the parameter's own position (`5:6-9`) and at its use (`5:44-47`) — a message about the `label` expression rather than about the name, which is the part worth recording. Workaround: rename (`tag`). |
| 9 | a two-line `a \|\| b` does not parse, with the `\|\|` at the end of the first line or at the start of the second: the lexer takes a run of vertical lines as an encloser, not as a continued infix operator | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/lexical-structure.tex:1176-1178` (a run of ≥2 vertical lines is a base operator); `basic/operators/enclosingops.tex:24-33` (the fixity table's *line break* row: no whitespace before ⇒ right encloser) | `fable-review-probes/RvwBarEnd.fss`; `g26` (leading form) | blinded, run-b | `Unmatched delimiter "component"` for the trailing form; `Syntax Error` at the `\|\|` for the leading form. run-b's 9′ closed this row's "not tried": the leading form now is tried, and both fail. The workaround is to parenthesise the whole expression, which allows a break anywhere inside it (`run-b/probes/c17_bar_continuation.fss`: `<\|[\String\] "a", "b" \|> \|\|` / `<\|[\String\] "c" \|>` inside parentheses prints `<\|a, b, c\|>`). |
| 10 | `do … end` **is** allowed as a comprehension body | RETIRED | — | — | `g11` | blinded (claimed the opposite) | `<\|[\ZZ32\] do i + 1 end \| i <- 0#3 \|>` prints `<\|1, 2, 3\|>`. Falsifies half of blinded gap #14; the `\|\|` half (row 9) stands. |
| 11 | objects may not declare varargs constructor parameters | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/objects.tex:66` ("varargs parameters are eliminated"); `fortress/preamble.tex:63` | `fable-review-probes/RvwVarargsObj.fss` | blinded | `Varargs parameters of objects are not allowed.` Use a factory function (varargs *functions* work — `spec-probes/p12_multifix.fss`). |
| 12 | static arguments inside an array literal (`[\T\ …]`) are in the spec grammar but not parsed | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | `basic/expressions/aggregate.tex:27` | `libvector-probes/p27_literal_staticargs.fss` | ours | `Unmatched delimiters "[\" and "]".` |
| 13 | import aliasing is unusable: the spec's `as` form is a parse error, the implementation's `=>` form parses but binds nothing | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | `basic/components/source-code.tex:93` (`AliasedSimpleName ::= Id [as Id]`) | `alias-units-probes/p10_import_as.fss`, `p12_import_arrow.fss` | ours | `Missing comma.` / `Seq is undefined.` |
| 84 | a superscript or postfix operator may not follow a dotted access: `x.v^T`, `B.data^T`, `B.n^2`, `B.data^*`, and the same after a getter or a method call, all give a bare `Syntax Error` at the `^` | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | `appendices/grammars/concrete-syntax.tex:947-952` (`Primary . Id`, `Primary ^ Exponent` and `Primary ExponentOp` are all `Primary` productions); `basic/operators/juxtameaning.tex:137-142` (a dotted chain is a selector chain, not a barrier) | `run-b/probes/c20b_field_transpose.fss`; `run-b2/probes/g4a_base.fss`, `g4a_getter.fss`, `g4a_call.fss`, `g4a_num.fss`, `g4a_expop2.fss` | run-b, run-b2 | Mechanism (run-b2's, checked here): `PrimaryItem` tries `LeftAssociatedPrimary` before `MathPrimary` (`Expression.rats:384-386`), and `LeftAssociatedPrimary` (`:388-434`) admits `Subscripting*`, `ParenthesisDelimited*` and `Selector*` but no `MathItem`; exponentiation is a `MathItem` (`Exponentiation`, `:651-656`) and `MathItem`s attach only to a `MathPrimary` (`:463-465`), which a dotted form never becomes. A *subscript* after a dot is fine (`B.data[5]` = `15`, `g4a_sub`). Workaround `(x.v)^T` (`run-b/probes/c20_pi_and_syntax.fss` (2a)). Rows 1-3's neighbour: same grammar, different parser path. |
| 85 | a prefix operator application as the second operand of a juxtaposition (`"text " SQRT(d)`, `std SQRT(x) cos(y)`) is a syntax error | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/operators/juxtameaning.tex:145-178` — the reassociation procedure cannot place a bare operator token inside a juxtaposition (`:156-162`, as in row 4) | `run-b/probes/c20c_prefix_juxtaposition.fss` (fails), `c20_pi_and_syntax.fss` (3a) (works) | run-b | `Syntax Error`. Workaround: parenthesize — `std (SQRT(x)) (cos(y))` prints `2.0`. |
| 86 | `(x DOT x)/\|x\|` does not lex: `/\|` is taken as one operator token | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/lexical-structure.tex:1176-1178` (a run of operator characters is one token) | `g20` | run-b | `Unmatched delimiters "(" and "/\|".` Workaround `/(\|x\|)`, which is also the form Fortify sets as a stacked fraction. |
| 87 | "a value juxtaposed with a bar-enclosed expression (`w \|hs\|`) is a syntax error" | RETIRED | — | `basic/operators/enclosingops.tex:24-33` (fixity table: left context *primary tail*, whitespace before *yes*, after *no* ⇒ **left encloser**) | `run-b2/probes/g4c_bars_bare.fss`, `g4c_bars_first.fss` | run-b2 (its own first reading) | It works: `w \|hs\| = 15`, and as an operand, as an argument, in conditions, and with the bar expression first (`\|hs\| w = 15`). Only the tight form fails — row 88. Recorded so the misreading of the fixity table is not made again. |
| 88 | the tight form `w\|hs\|`, with no space anywhere, does fail — and the fixity table says it should | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/operators/enclosingops.tex:24-33` (same table row: whitespace before *no*, after *no* ⇒ **infix**) | `run-b2/probes/g4c_bars_tight.fss` | run-b2 | `Resolution of operator property failed for: … Right encloser without left encloser.`, the resolver's dump showing `TightInfix: op = \|`: the first bar was taken as an infix operator, leaving the second unmatched. Workaround: a space before the bar. A different claim from row 9, which is about a line-final `\|\|`. |
| 89 | `-` and `AND` have incomparable precedence, so an expression mixing them across a comparison needs **both** halves parenthesised | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/operators/precedence.tex:162-169` — "operator precedence in Fortress is not always transitive; … the expression \EXP{a \vee b + c} is not permitted" | `run-b2/probes/g4f_bare.fss` (fails), `g4f_both.fss` (works) | run-b2 | `Loose operators - and AND have incomparable precedence.` from `Resolver.java:598-601`. The check is on *adjacent* operators in the flat infix chain, so any grouping that removes the adjacency is accepted; with no arithmetic in the operands no parentheses are needed at all. Met again by the APL rungs with another pair: `(i + k) MOD rows + rows` is `Loose operators MOD and + have incomparable precedence`, and the fix is the fully parenthesised `((((i + k) MOD rows) + rows) MOD rows)` that `aplRotN` carries (`apl/rung-3/t08_prec.fss` + `.out.0`/`.out`, re-run in the APL merge; apl gaps row 66, merged here). |
| 90 | a postfix operator declaration whose operator is `!` needs a space before the return-type colon: `opr (A: Mat)!: Mat` is rejected, `opr (A: Mat)! : Mat` is accepted | NEGATIVE-BOUNDED | implementation gap | `basic/lexical-structure.tex:1176-1186` (a base operator is one ordinary operator character, so `!:` lexes as one token); `appendices/grammars/concrete-syntax.tex` (`OpDef`) | `run-b2/probes/g4a_postfix.fss` (fails), `g4a_postfix2.fss` (works) | run-b2 | `Invalid top-level declaration.` spanning the whole `opr` line, versus `101` from the spaced form. Only these two spellings were tried; `^T` needs no space (`g4a_base`'s declaration parses), so the trap is specific to ordinary operator characters. Same family as row 63. |
| 91 | `b.f(3)`, where `f` is a field holding a function, is a **method invocation** by specification, not an application of the field — and the interpreter's diagnostic for it is an internal bug report | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit (the diagnostic: implementation gap) | `basic/operators/juxtameaning.tex:137-142` — "If the last identifier in the dotted id chain is part of a selector … and it is immediately followed by a left parenthesis, then the last selector together with the subsequent parenthesis-delimited expression is a method invocation" | `run-b2/probes/g4d_dotcall.fss` (fails), `g4d_loose.fss` (works) | run-b2, blinded (its row 12) | `** bug! The number of parameters (0) does not match with the number of arguments (1).` — the getter for `f` is invoked with one argument. The program is erroneous by the spec, so the gap is the diagnostic. Workarounds, all confirmed: `b.f (3)` with a space, `(b.f)(3)`, or bind first. |
| 92 | a local name may not repeat a top-level function name; a *field* of an object may shadow a top-level variable | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED + POSITIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/declarations.tex:476-533` — the field/dotted-method case is the permitted shadowing (`:482`), and "No other shadowing is permitted" (`:533`) | `g21`; `run-b/probes/c16_model_spellings.fss` (7) | run-b | `Variable step is already declared.` — Karpathy's loop variable `step` collides with a function named `step` (also seen for `model`, `rows`). `object Adam(epsilon: RR64)` beside a top-level `epsilon` is fine. |
| 93 | a prime-marked identifier (`X'`) is a legal name | POSITIVE-VERIFIED | — | `basic/lexical-structure.tex:351-352` (identifier characters include prime characters and apostrophe); `appendices/rendering.tex` renders them as primes | `run-b2/probes/p_names.fss` | run-b2 | `X' = 4.0` alongside `d_k = 4`, `epsilon_adam = 1.0E-8`, `lr_t = 0.01`, `SQRT d_k = 2.0`. The mathematical register for "the updated value of X"; contrast row 15, where `W_1` is not a name at all. |
| 143 | the wildcard `_` is not an assignment target: `(a, _) := pair()` is a `Syntax Error`; the tuple **binding** `(a, _) = pair()` and the tuple assignment `(a, b) := pair()` both work | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED + POSITIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `appendices/grammars/concrete-syntax.tex:916-919` (`AssignLeft ::= SubscriptExpr \| FieldSelection \| QualifiedName`) | `run-c/probes/p07b_tupleassign.fss` (fails), `p07a_tupleassign.fss`, `g143_wild.fss` (work) | run-c | `Syntax Error` at the underscore's own column (`p07b_tupleassign.fss:7:12`); the binding prints `a = 1.5` and the assignment prints `1.5 2.5` and then the swap `2.5 1.5`. The grammar admits no wildcard on the left of `:=`. Workaround: a throwaway variable. |
| 144 | a function call immediately followed by a postfix or superscript operator (`onehot(ids, vocabSize)^T`) is the static error of row 4: `the argument should not be immediately followed by a non-expression element` | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/operators/juxtameaning.tex:156-162` (as row 4) | `run-c/probes/g144_callsuper.fss` | run-c | `Syntax Error: the argument should not be immediately followed by a non-expression element.` at the `^`. Row 4's rule met from the other side — a superscript instead of a subscript — and the same relation to row 4 that row 3 has to row 1. The probe's working half prints `(1) bound first: (a^T).sizes = 2 3`. Workaround: bind the result first — `oh = onehot(…); (oh^T) dX`. |

Run C cited its own `p03_zz64.fss` for the `big` half, but that probe no longer contains the name, so `g29.fss` re-establishes it: a plain `big: ZZ32 = 10 x` inside a top-level function gives `Variable big is already declared.` (`big(self):ZZ` is a functional method of `ZZ`, `FortressLibrary.fsi:530`). Row 92 extended from top-level functions to the method names of every trait in scope: inside the object body they are bare names. Workaround: rename (`ij`, `bigv`); grep `Library/FortressLibrary.fsi` for the name before choosing it. |
| 158 | a parenthesised call followed by `^T` — `(onehot(ks, 5))^T dx`, and `((onehot(ks, 5))^T) dx` — is still row 144's error: row 4's workaround `(f())[i]` does not carry over to a superscript, though the grammar makes a parenthesis-delimited expression a `Primary` that an `ExponentOp` may follow | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit (row 144 is the rule; this is the parenthesised form, which it also refuses) | `basic/expressions/operator-app.tex:29-32` (`Primary ::= LeftEncloser … RightEncloser \| Primary ^ Exponent \| Primary ExponentOp`); `basic/operators/juxtameaning.tex:156-162` (the sentence the message quotes, whose "argument" here is inside the parentheses) | `run-c/probes/q04f_callsuper.fss` (3), `q04a_varargs.fss` (5) (fail), `q04f_callsuper.fss` (1), (2) (work); `run-c3/probes/lift_g4.fss` (fails), `lift_g4b.fss` (bind first: works) | run-c2, run-c3 | Re-run here, all four: `Syntax Error: a function argument should not be immediately followed by a non-expression element.`, with the span on the **call** and not on the parentheses (`q04f_callsuper.fss:18:48-59`, `q04a_varargs.fss:35:45-56`, `lift_g4.fss:11:11-22`), so the enclosing parentheses are not seen at all. `transpose(onehot(ks, 5)) dx` and `(onehot(ks, 5)).t() dx` print the right product, and binding first — `oh = onehot(…)` then `(oh^T) dX` — works (`lift_g4b`: `oh^T sizes = (5,3)`). | 167 | a top-level value may not share its name with a `nat` static parameter of a function declared in the same component: with `q` at top level, `f[\nat r, nat c, nat p, nat q\](…)` is `Variable q is already declared.` at the generic's declaration | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit (row 147's no-shadowing rule, met from the static side) | `basic/declarations.tex:476-533` (as rows 92, 147: ":533" is "No other shadowing is permitted") | `run-c3/probes/att_g_staticname.fss` | run-c3 | Re-run here: `att_g_staticname.fss:6:38: Variable q is already declared.` — line 6 is the `assignInto[\nat r, nat c, nat p, nat q\](…)` declaration, column 38 the `q` of `nat q`, and `q` is the top-level value on line 5. So the static-parameter names of every generic in a component share one namespace with its top-level values. Bit Run C3 twice more (functions named `rows`/`block` against parameters of the same name) and Run C once (row 147). Workaround: rename the static parameter. |
| 168 | row 92's rule holds inside **api** files too: an api that declares `rows(n: ZZ32): ZZ32` and `area(rows: ZZ32, cols: ZZ32): ZZ32` fails at the parameter with `Variable rows is already declared.`, reported at the api file's own line | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/declarations.tex:476-533` (as rows 92, 147, 167) | `run-c3/probes/g164_api_param_name/UseG164.fss` (run from that directory) | run-c3 | Re-run here: `G164.fsi:3:6-8: Variable rows is already declared.` — `G164.fsi:3` is `area(rows: ZZ32, cols: ZZ32): ZZ32` and columns 6-8 are its parameter, against `rows(n: ZZ32): ZZ32` on line 2. The component carries the same clash, so the probe does not show that the api is checked first, only that the diagnostic is located in the `.fsi`. | 193 | a nonterminal's name may not be a word of **two or more uppercase letters**: such a word is an operator, not an identifier, and the api is rejected with a bare `Syntax Error` pointing at the name. Re-run in this merge, output as committed, both lines identical to the `transpose` spelling. |

**Precedence verdict (rows 1-4).** Fortress does leave precedence undefined
between some operator families and then demands parentheses
(`basic/operators/precedence.tex:26-32`, the `a + b ∪ c` example), but that rule
does not reach `x[i][j]`, `x[i]^2` or `f()[i]`. Subscripting and superscripting
are one group that `precedence.tex:44-51` explicitly declares left-associative and
higher than every operator below, and `concrete-syntax.tex:937-953` is left-recursive
on `Primary` for both forms; the parser agrees (`Expression.rats:389, 516-543, 627-648`,
and the dumped AST carries both subscripts). So `x[i][j]` and `x[i]^2` are an
**implementation gap**, localised to one argument-list call in the evaluator.
`f()[i]` is the opposite verdict: the grammar admits it, but the spec's own
reassociation procedure (`juxtameaning.tex:156-162`) makes it a static error, and
the interpreter reports that sentence — a **design limit**.

Deleting `interpreter_cache` alone does not restore it; deleting the `interpreter_parsed_cache` entry does. Practical consequence: an overload error can be invisible to its author and fatal on a clean checkout — and every declaration-time verdict in this ledger must be taken cold. Met again by the APL rungs from the other side, where a cold cache makes a program fail that succeeds ever after: the first component to import `AplCore` **directly** is rejected for a conflict between one of its free operators and a generic trait method of the shipped library (`AdditiveGroup.+` at `Library/FortressLibrary.fss:330`, `StandardTotalOrder.MAX` at `:280`), and the second run of the same unchanged file succeeds (`apl/rung-5/v04_lib.fss` + `.out.0`/`.out`, re-run in the APL merge; apl gaps row 82, merged here). A component that imports the grammar api as well never trips it, because compiling the grammar puts `AplCore` in the cache first. Practical consequence recorded there: after wiping `default_repository/caches/*`, run a library-only probe twice. |

Workaround: a `fill` over an index map (`run-b2/probes/g1d_hcat.fss`, which also splits back: `round trip hcat then hsplit identical: true`). Mechanism: `IndexedArrayWrapper` swaps the first two indices on every access (`IndexedArrayWrapper.java:54` and `:79`, both commented `This "fixes" the row-column confusion in pasted arrays`), so a block is read transposed — the swap cancels for square blocks and runs off the end otherwise. |

Workaround: `hsplit` by `fill`. |

Workaround: a user `ODOT` (⊙) on the carrier, or `ivmap`. The APL rungs priced the absence: `m + n` and `m - n` between two runtime-built matrices need nothing declared (`Matrix extends AdditiveGroup`), while APL's elementwise `×`, `÷` and `*` cost three declarations each — `(M,M)`, `(s,M)`, `(M,s)` (`apl/rung-3/t01_ops.fss` + `.out` (e), `t04_lib.out`, re-run in the APL merge; apl gaps row 64, merged here). The absence is lucky for APL, whose `×` is elementwise: the library's matrix product is the method `mul`, so the operator was free. |

## 7. Parallelism and mutation

| # | claim | status | class | spec citation | reproducer | found by | notes / workaround |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 59 | a bare `acc := acc + c` inside a parallel `for` **silently loses updates**; `atomic do … end` is correct; the spec's own `acc += e` reduction-variable form is unimplemented | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | `basic/evaluation/reduction.tex:14-17`; `fortress/preamble.tex:71` | `matrix-ad-probes/p11.fss` at `FORTRESS_THREADS=4` | ours | Expected `5050 10100 15150`; three runs gave `2968 …`, `1505 …`, `3213 …`. Invisible at one thread. The "`atomic do … end` is correct" clause was measured with a local variable (`matrix-ad-probes/p11.fss:46-50` declares `var acc` inside `run`) and was false for a variable declared at the top level of a component on the compiled path until the repair batch's R1: such a variable was read and written outside the transaction, so `atomic` lost updates there too (`compiler_tests/AtomicTopLevelVar`, 37826 of 40000 with `FORTRESS_THREADS` unset on `49ee5e91`; exact at one thread, which is what the gate runs at). |
| 60 | reduction expressions and `__generate` are parallel-safe at 4 threads | POSITIVE-VERIFIED | — | `basic/evaluation/reduction.tex:15-17` | `matrix-ad-probes/p11.fss` at 4 threads | ours | Rows (b), (c), (d) identical across three runs. |
| 61 | `spawn` and `.val()` work on the interpreter path | POSITIVE-VERIFIED | — | `appendices/grammars/concrete-syntax.tex:982` | `compiler-probes/p32.fss` run interpreted | ours | `p32 42`. Contrast row 74. |
| 111 | `for c <- s` over a `String` is a **parallel, unordered** generator: at one thread it silently prints the string backwards | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | design limit | `basic/expressions/for.tex:28-32` — "the programmer must assume that each loop iteration will occur independently in parallel unless every generator is explicitly \VAR{sequential}" | `run-b2/probes/g4i_order.fss` | run-b2 | `for ch <- uchars` over `"abcde"` prints `edcba`; `for ch <- seq(uchars)` and `for i <- seq(0 # \|uchars\|)` print `abcde`. Specified behaviour, but a stable reversal rather than anything obviously parallel, which is how it slips past a reader. Any character-level pass — tokenising, building a vocabulary — must wrap the generator in `seq`. Row 59's trap family. |
| 155 | a parallel `for d <- 0#bsz, h <- 0#nHead` whose iterations write disjoint blocks of shared matrices through views, with reads of shared matrices, gives the same results at `FORTRESS_THREADS=4` as at 1 | POSITIVE-VERIFIED | — | `basic/expressions/for.tex:28-32` | `run-c/checks/threads4.txt` against `threads1.txt` (the attention loops of `run-c/src/MicroGptFlat.fss`); `run-c3/probes/att_b.fss`, `lift_c.fss`, `run-c3/checks/threads1.txt` against `threads4.txt` | run-c, run-c3 | Verified here by diffing the two committed check outputs rather than by re-running the 855 s check: the only differing lines are the header (`threads 1` / `threads 4`), the six lines that carry an elapsed time, and the final total — every loss, gradient difference, Adam difference and finite difference is identical to the last digit, and both files show 36 PASS and 0 FAIL. So the library's parallel `SUM` and product associate the same way at both thread counts. Row 59's trap avoided by construction: nothing is accumulated across iterations; the products and softmaxes allocate per iteration and `assignInto` writes each cell once. Four threads: 2.6-3.0 s per batch-1 step against 7.7-7.9, 8.6 s per batch-4 step against 26.6, 353 s for the check against 855. Run C3's independent port extends the row to two more shapes, both re-run here at 1 and 4 threads, and was merged into it rather than entered: a parallel `for d <- 0#bsz, h <- 0#nHead` whose iterations each store a whole matrix into a slot of a shared `array[\Any\](bsz nHead)` (`att_b.fss:96-97`), and a parallel `for i <- 0#nr` writing rows of a shared matrix through `Row` views (`lift_c.fss:52`). At `FORTRESS_THREADS=4` against 1 the only differing lines of either probe are the timing lines — every reported sum and element identical to the last digit — and its check's two outputs agree likewise, both `36 PASS, 0 FAIL of 36`. The speed-up here: ten `rows(rmsn, x)` 1712 ms against 617 ms, and ten forward-and-backward repetitions of `att_b` 28.1 s against 12.5 s. |
| 265 | the **frame stack is unsafe under the language's implicit parallelism**: the elements of a tuple expression are evaluated in parallel in separate implicit threads, so `¨` over a strand written as the tuple `(aplCall1(f, a), aplCall1(f, b), aplCall1(f, c))` pushes three frames at once onto the one stack; at `FORTRESS_THREADS=4` the model dies reading a frame before it is written ("Access to uninitialized element 1 of array PrimitiveArray[\Any,1024\]") and the check with "Index -1 out of bounds for length 1024"; at pool size 1 the implicit threads run in sequence and nothing shows | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED (the race), POSITIVE-VERIFIED (the fix) | design limit (of the frame-stack design, row 245, against the language's parallel evaluation) | `Specification/basic/expressions/tuple-expr.tex:23-24` (each element of a tuple is evaluated in parallel); `basic/expressions/operator-app.tex:59` (operands likewise) | `apl/microgpt/checks/model_run_threads4.out.0`, `apl/microgpt/checks/threads4_crash.txt` (the crashes); `apl/base/AplCore.fss` (`aplEachT2`, `aplEachT3` sequenced in a block); `apl/microgpt/checks/model_run_threads4.out`, `apl/microgpt/checks/threads4.txt` (after) | apl microgpt rung | The base's rung-4 comment assumed `FORTRESS_THREADS=1`; the assumption was never exercised because every rung ran at pool size 1. The fix sequences the two sites the base controls; the limit stands wherever the host evaluates two APL calls in parallel — the components of a tuple, the arguments of a call, the operands of an operator — and a program that puts two dfn applications in one such group still races. This program has no other such group |

## 10. Bytecode-compiler path (a different execution path)

Verified by `fortress compile` (+ `fortress run` where the failure is at run time),
each against the same source run through the interpreter. Rows 307-310 are the exception:
they need the compiler's phase order over the *interpreter's* prelude, which
`perf-probes/prelude/WorldFlip.java` sets up through `Shell`'s public entry points, with
an instrumented copy of `StaticChecker` first on the classpath so that the pass can be
read past its first casualty. No tracked file was changed to get them.

| 331 | **the corpus and the interpreter's prelude spell an optional type's empty case as a parametric `Nothing[\T\]`, which the specification does not sanction and the compiler world therefore does not accept.** `Specification/basic-lib/convenience.tex:49` — prose, not an api rendering — declares it as one non-parametric object, `object Nothing extends Maybe[\T\] excludes Just[\T\] where {T extends Object}`, and `basic/exceptions.tex:122,124` use it that way, `getter message(): Maybe[\String\] = Nothing`. The interpreter's `value object Nothing[\T\]` (`Library/FortressLibrary.fss:1342`) is a deliberate workaround, and its own source says so in the comment immediately above it (`:1340-1341`, "Obviously ought to be a non-parametric singleton when we get where clauses working"); 22 files in `ProjectFortress/tests/` and the `*_tests/` directories, plus `Library/List.fss`, `Set.fss`, `Map.fss`, `PureList.fss` and `QuickCheck.fss`/`.fsi`, write the parametric form. Rung M gave the compiler prelude the specification's spelling (`Library/CompilerLibrary.fsi:217-234`), so on that path `Nothing[\ZZ32\]` is now "Unexpected type for a singleton object reference". The two files it costs are `tests/ExceptionScoping.fss:31` (`Nothing[\ZZ32\].get`) and `tests/oddJuxt.fss:22,24`. What is measured of them, and all that is measured: `ExceptionScoping.fss` carried one error before the rung and carries one after, `oddJuxt.fss` carried two and carries one, and after the rung that remaining error is this spelling — "Unexpected type for a singleton object reference" at `ExceptionScoping.fss:31:13-26` and at `oddJuxt.fss:22:9-21` (`compile-ladder/rung-maybe/ladder-after/raw/tests/`). Neither reached a run either way, so neither is shown to be a compile away from passing; no file in the rung's six-file subset reaches `pass`. the statement above narrowed by this probe, which contradicts the skeptic's reading) | **The reading of the message, not a verified mechanism.** The arrow descriptor the code generator writes carries the void marker `Naming.INTERNAL_SNOWMAN` only when the parameter list is syntactically empty — `NamingCzar.makeAnArrowDescriptor`, `ProjectFortress/src/com/sun/fortress/compiler/NamingCzar.java:1166`, the `else` of `if (params.size() > 0)` — so a parameter whose type is the static parameter `T` is written as an ordinary one-argument arrow. At class-instantiation time the void special cases in `ProjectFortress/src/com/sun/fortress/runtimeSystem/InstantiatingClassloader.java:597`, `:1002`, `:1109` and `:1222` all key on the arrow's first parameter **being** that marker, which after substituting `T := ()` it now is, and they drop the argument from `apply`. The emitted class and the instantiated interface then disagree on `apply`'s arity, which is exactly what the `AbstractMethodError` names. All four special cases live in the runtime instantiator and none in `CodeGen`, so the two halves cannot agree by construction. **A repair is gated by nothing**: no `.test` file exercises a void static argument — `library_tests/MaybeRungM.fss` cannot, since `filter` is the member it does not reach (rung M's skeptic, `SKEPTIC.md:136`) — so the fix needs a new gated test as well. Two corollaries measured alongside: `()` satisfies the bound `Any` but not the default bound `Object`, so a user-declared `object Box[\T\]` refuses `Box[\()\]` as a static error (`ArrD.fss`, `ArrE.fss`, `Ill-formed type … The static argument () does not satisfy the corresponding bound Object`) and only the `extends Any` spelling the preludes use reaches the defect at all; and the interpreter is correct on every one of these programs, so this is a compiled-path defect with no interpreter counterpart. |
| 340 | **a `typecase` used directly as a function or method body, whose value needs an implicit coercion, crashes the code generator**: `f(o: S): ZZ32 = typecase o of A => 1 else => 0 end` — where the clause bodies are integer literals and the declared return type is `ZZ32`, so the result is coerced from `IntLiteral` — makes `fortress compile` die with `java.lang.Error: … Error trying to close method scope` and `rc=1`, a crash with a bytecode dump rather than a diagnosed static error, while `bin/fortress` walk runs the identical program and prints `1`. The wrapped cause, from the capture, is `java.lang.ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException: Index 1 out of bounds for length 1` at `org.objectweb.asm.Frame.merge(Frame.java:1281)` ← `Frame.merge(:1256)` ← `MethodWriter.computeAllFrames(MethodWriter.java:1612)` ← `MethodWriter.visitMaxs(:1548)` ← `CodeGenMethodVisitor.visitMaxs(ProjectFortress/src/com/sun/fortress/compiler/codegen/CodeGenMethodVisitor.java:99)` ← `CodeGen.methodReturnAndFinish(ProjectFortress/src/com/sun/fortress/compiler/codegen/CodeGen.java:3408)` ← `CodeGen.exitMethodScope(:3045)` ← `CodeGen.generateActualMethodCode(:2800)`, whose `catch (Throwable t)` at `:2801-2802` is what turns it into the `Error` and prints the method's bytecode — so nothing was "closing" in the Fortress sense: the failure is ASM's stack-map frame computation on the method the generator had just emitted | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap (code generation) | `Specification/basic/functions.tex:46-48` makes a function body any expression (`FnDecl ::= [FnMods] FnHeaderFront FnHeaderClause = Expr`); `Specification/basic/expressions/typecase.tex:53-55` makes `typecase` an ordinary `DelimitedExpr` with `CaseElse` optional (`:73`) and `:110-111` gives it "the union of types of all right-hand sides of the typecase clauses" — so the program is exactly what the grammar and the typing rule sanction | `explorations/compile-ladder/rung-maybe/probes/typecase-body/` (to be committed from this probe's scratchpad): `TcPlain.fss` is the minimal reproducer (three non-generic declarations and one function; `compile-rc=1`, `walk` prints `TcPlain=1`), with the full cause chain in `captures/TcPlain.txt`; `TcNoElse.fss` and `TcMethod.fss` are the same crash without `else` and as a method body; the controls are `TcInDo.fss`, `TcNoLit.fss`, `TcVoid.fss` and `TcIfCtl.fss`, all four `compile-rc=0` and correct at run time. The sighting that started it was `OptFullExpl.fss`, `OptEmptyExpl3.fss` and `OptFull2.fss` (generic objects in the clauses — incidental, not the trigger) | ours (this probe, 2026-09-19; met while probing static-argument inference for the `Maybe` fork) | **What the dump shows, as a reading and not a verified mechanism.** The `Error`'s own bytecode text (`captures/TcPlain.txt`) has, after the `else` branch's `GOTO L6`, an unreachable `INVOKESTATIC …FVoid.make ()` — the `typecase`'s no-match path — and then at `L6` the coercion block `DUP` / `INVOKESTATIC ConcreteTuple?fortress/CompilerBuiltin$IntLiteral?.isA` / `GETSTATIC TcPlain$CoercionDesugarer#0.ONLY`. Two paths reach `L6` with different operand stacks, which is what `Frame.merge` refuses. The controls agree with that reading: remove the coercion consumer at the join (`TcNoLit`, `TcVoid`) or put a block between it and the `typecase` (`TcInDo`) and the method verifies. **It is ungated, and cheaply gated.** Every use of this shape in `compiler_tests` returns `()` and so never reaches the coercion — `Compiled5.o.fss:17` and `Compiled5.p.fss:17` (`run(): () = typecase x of`), `Compiled5.q.fss:18`, `Compiled5.r.fss:15`, `Compiled5.s.fss:15`, `Compiled5.t.fss:18`, `Compiled5.bi.fss:21` (`quux(obj: A):() = typecase obj of`) — and `Compiled6.av.fss:16`, `:41` and `Compiled6.bl.fss:18` use `b := typecase a of`, an assignment inside a block, which is the `TcInDo` shape. Adding `TcPlain`'s four lines to a gated test is the whole cost. **Relation to row 79**: same family, different defect. Row 79 is `typecase` on an `IntLiteral` *subject* silently taking the wrong branch (`compiler-probes/p33.fss`); this row is `typecase` producing an `IntLiteral` *result* that the declared return type coerces, and crashing the compiler. A repair to the literal's typing would touch both; neither subsumes the other. The workaround is the one-token `do … end`. |

| # | claim | status | class | spec citation | reproducer | found by | notes / workaround |

| 112 | a `value` object may declare a `var` field; the interpreter accepts it, mutates it in place, and the object then aliases like a reference object | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | `basic/objects.tex:33-34` ("A value object cannot have mutable fields"), `:356-357` ("The fields of a \KWD{value} object are immutable"), `:38` ("an implementation may freely copy value objects") | `run-b2/probes/g1a_varfield.fss`, `g1a_copy.fss` | run-b2 | `value object Counter(var n: ZZ32)` with `n := n + 1` prints `built Counter(1)` / `after bump Counter(2)`; no diagnostic at any phase. `d = c` then `c.bump()` gives `c = VCounter(2)   d = VCounter(2)`, so the copying licence is not taken either. Nothing enforces valueness of *objects*; what is enforced is the trait rule (row 115). |
| 113 | `SEQV` (`===`) on two value objects compares only the fields the object *body* declares — with none, it compares nothing and answers `true` for unequal objects | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | `basic/objects.tex:434-436` — "the result is \VAR{true} if and only if corresponding fields of the objects are themselves equivalent" | `run-b2/probes/g1a_equality2.fss`, `g1a_equality.fss`; `run-b/probes/worker/w09_valueobj_scalar.fss`, `w09_valueobj.fss` | run-b2, run-b | `VPlain(1,2) SEQV VPlain(1,3) = true` and `P(1.0) === P(2.0) : true`, while `1.0 === 2.0 : false`; one dummy field declaration in the body fixes it (`VField(1,2) SEQV VField(1,3) = false`). Mechanism (run-b2's, checked here): `FOrdinaryObject.seqv` (`FOrdinaryObject.java:46-57`) walks `ftype.getFieldNames()`, and `FTypeObject`'s constructor adds the constructor parameters to `fields` **inside** the `if (v instanceof VarDecl)` branch (`FTypeObject.java:53-63`), so a body with no field declaration leaves the list empty. run-b read the same output as "`===` compares only the dynamic type"; run-b2's mechanism is the right reading of it. |

Any code reaching for `SEQV` on a `Maybe` — the natural strict-equality test, and `indexOf` returns one (row 138) — silently gets a constant `true`. |
| 115 | value traits work, and the one valueness rule that *is* enforced is the trait rule: a reference object extending a value trait is rejected | POSITIVE-VERIFIED + NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | — | `basic/traits.tex:809-812`; the chapter note at `basic/traits.tex:15-16` says value traits are "not yet supported" | `run-b2/probes/g1a_valtrait.fss`, `g1a_valtrait_bad.fss` | run-b2 | The good case runs (`a.area + b.area = 18.0`, `Sq(3.0) SEQV Sq(3.0) = true`); `object Bad(s: RR64) extends Shape` gives `Bad: non-value subtype of value type Shape`. Corrects the spec's own chapter note, which is stale for this tree. Note the inherited-getter effect in the good case: both instances print as `Shape(area=9.0)`. |
| 116 | the spec's `settable` abbreviation on a value object works: `z.im := 7.0` rebuilds the object | POSITIVE-VERIFIED | — | `basic/objects.tex:359-366` and the `Complex` example at `:370-375` | `run-b2/probes/g1a_settable.fss` | run-b2 | `value object Cpx(settable re: RR64, settable im: RR64)`: `z = (1.0 + 2.0 i)`, after `z.im := 7.0` `z = (1.0 + 7.0 i)`, `z + z = (2.0 + 14.0 i)`. The receiver must be a `var`. This is the shipped path to the spec's own `Complex` — the standing complex-numbers goal. |
| 117 | a value object carrying an `Array[\RR64,(ZZ32,ZZ32)\]` field, with `opr +` and `opr juxtaposition` as functional methods, works, and its extents can be recovered from the array alone | POSITIVE-VERIFIED | — | `basic/objects.tex:347-352`; `basic/functions.tex` (functional methods) | `run-b2/probes/g1a_valobj.fss`, `g1a_valobj_arrayonly.fss` | run-b2 | `A + B = [ 2.0 3.5 ; 2.5 5.0 ;]` and `A B = [ 2.5 3.5 ; 4.0 5.0 ;]`. `a.bounds.extent.get` recovers the shape, so the carrier needs neither `nat` parameters nor stored extents — the escape from rows 23 and 25 that run-b2's `Mat` is built on. |

| 140 | the library's `strToFloat` mis-parses a sign and overflows on a long mantissa: `strToFloat "-1.5"` is `-28.5`, and a 17-digit mantissa dies with `Overflow of ZZ32 100000000000000000` | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | library bug | — (the spec has no `strToFloat`; `Library/FortressLibrary.fss:4192-4197`, whose comment says "Quick and dirty implementation") | `run-c/probes/g140_strtofloat.fss` | run-c | Re-run here: `(1) strToFloat "1.5" = 1.5`, `(2) strToFloat "-1.5" = -28.5`, then `FortressLibrary.fss:4196:85-94: Overflow of ZZ32 100000000000000000`. Mechanism, from the one-line body `SUM[k <- 0 # \|both\|] 10^k (both[\|both\|-1-k].codePoint - '0'.codePoint)`: every character, sign included, is a digit of value `codePoint - '0'.codePoint`, so `-` counts as -3 and `"-1.5"` is (-3*100 + 1*10 + 5)/10 = -28.5; and `10^k` is `ZZ32`, which overflows at the 10th digit before the mantissa is even summed. Workaround: the 25-line `parseFloat` in `run-c/src/MicroGptFlat.fss` (digits into an `RR64` mantissa, an exact power of ten, `-` and the high minus, an `e`/`E` exponent with either sign). |

Workaround: scan characters (`parseRow` in `run-c/src/MicroGptFlat.fss`, `numbersOf` in its check). |

Verified in this merge on the **retired first library**, from a git worktree of `0ff5eae27~1` (the retired library is that commit's `explorations/apl/v1/base`, its probes that commit's `v1/rung-1`, `v1/rung-2`, `v1/rung-2b`, kept here as `apl/v1-probes/`): re-run output as committed. The third citation was `../v1/rung-1/Rung1.out` before the 2026-09-13 retirement's sed replaced it with the words “v1 (retired; git history)” in `apl/gaps.md`; it is restored here and re-ran as committed. |

(64x16)^T) 890 ms against 1098 ms | POSITIVE-VERIFIED (measurement) | design limit (of the walk interpreter) | — | `run-c/probes/p05_mmtime.fss` (second of two repetitions; the first is up to 2x slower) | run-c | Re-measured here; the numbers above are this host's, about 13 us per multiply-add. What holds unchanged is the shape of the result: the library product is the fastest of the four spellings, a hand-written loop is 1.4-1.5x slower, and cost tracks the multiply-add count rather than the shape, because the per-leaf overhead of the library's recursive partition and the per-iteration overhead of a `for` are the same order. Cheap fixes unchanged and now measured on the generated code: guard the `inATransaction` message with its flag; constant-fold the float-literal coercion (`FFloatLiteral` stores its value as a `String`, `FIntLiteral` keeps a `long`, which is why integer literals are cheap). The immutable spelling is a tail-recursive function in 20000 chunks of 1000, not a reduction: the compiled prelude has no `SUM` and no generic `BIG` (row 74), and the recurrence is sequential anyway. Not re-run in this merge (a 3-run benchmark); the committed `.out` files are the evidence. fixed (2026-09-17) |
| 306 | in Java over the runtime's own box shape, boxing costs **6.3× (a 4192-element dot product), 6.5× (a 16×16 matrix product) and 2.6× (the `rmsn` row lift)** against primitive `double[]`, and the **generic array object** — `Object[]` behind an interface with a `checkcast` per read, as `array[\RR64\](n)` is — adds only 9 %, 1.6 % and 4.8 % on top. The same boxes cost 1.7× in the scalar loop, because there they can be scalar-replaced and in an array they escape. The walk interpreter runs the same three kernels 5452×, 14376× and 2533× slower than primitive Java, and its profile is 43.5 % AST walk, 32.3 % environment lookup, 5.2 % run-time type dispatch, 0 % number representation | POSITIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | — | `perf-probes/kernels/java/`, `jfr/top-frames-interp-krows.txt`, `jfr/grouped-frames.txt` | ours (kernels probe) | Supports `performance-roadmap.md`'s A1/A2 (unbox by static type, `double[]`-backed arrays) for array-shaped code specifically, and withdraws the same argument for scalar code (rows 302, 303). The interpreter profile's top leaf frames are `StringHashComparer.compare` 12.4 %, `Evaluator.evalExprList` 6.0 %, `BATreeNode.rightWeightIncreased` 5.3 %: on the only path that runs microGPT-shaped code today, about three quarters of the time is name lookup in balanced-tree environments and the AST walk. Row 154's mechanism, measured from the runtime's side. Not re-run in this merge (a 3-run benchmark); the committed `.out` files are the evidence. |

## 16. Syntax extension: grammars, templates and sub-languages

<!-- New with the APL side quest (`explorations/apl/`), which climbed Kruger's
     "Learning APL" one chapter at a time inside `apl⦇ … ⦈` to find where the
     specification's syntax-extension mechanism gives out.  Rows about the host
     language proper are in the sections above; these are about the mechanism:
     the preparser, the grammar api, gaps, templates, hygiene and the shape a
     sub-language must take to survive them.  The paper the rows argue with is
     "Growing a Syntax" (Allen, Culpepper, Nielsen, Rafkind & Ryu, FOOL 2009;
     notes in `research/extracts/growing-a-syntax.md`), whose verdict
     each row records as agrees / disagrees / silent. -->

| # | claim | status | class | spec citation | reproducer | found by | notes / workaround |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 179 | whitespace in a grammar production is **optional** at the use site: a literal space between symbols and an explicit `SPACE` both translate to the nonterminal `w` | POSITIVE-VERIFIED | — | — (`Syntax.rats:343-345` lexes `SPACE`; `ComposingSyntaxDefTranslator.java:147-149` maps both it and a literal space to `w`) | `apl/v1-probes/p02b_mech.fss` | apl rung 1 | `nospace⦇5,5⦈` and `nospace⦇ 5 , 5 ⦈` both print `10`. This is what lets the book's `2 4⍴⍳8` parse against a production written with spaces, so the chapter's lines can be copied in verbatim. The converse is the limit: a sub-grammar cannot **require** whitespace, so APL strand notation rests on the greediness of `LiteralExpr`, not on `SPACE`. Verified in this merge on the **retired first library**, from a git worktree of `0ff5eae27~1` (the retired library is that commit's `explorations/apl/v1/base`, its probes that commit's `v1/rung-1`, `v1/rung-2`, `v1/rung-2b`, kept here as `apl/v1-probes/`): re-run output as committed. |
| 180 | one production serves `f/` for **every** function glyph: a glyph-table gap followed by the reduce slash, and the same with `⌿` | POSITIVE-VERIFIED | — | — | `apl/v1-probes/p02b_mech.fss`, `apl/base/AplSyntax.fsi` | apl rung 1 | `redop⦇ +/ 3 ⦈`, `⍳/`, `*/` and `redfi⦇ +⌿ 3 ⦈` all reduce to one call with the glyph's name. APL's reduce operator is therefore one rule, not one rule per glyph, even though the `?`-bound gap that would make the slash optional is unimplemented (row 274; the source row calls it “the merged ledger's apl-probes rows”, written while that feasibility table was still unmerged — it is rows 266-283 here). Verified in this merge on the **retired first library**, from a git worktree of `0ff5eae27~1` (the retired library is that commit's `explorations/apl/v1/base`, its probes that commit's `v1/rung-1`, `v1/rung-2`, `v1/rung-2b`, kept here as `apl/v1-probes/`): re-run output as committed. |
| 181 | a backtick escape in a production — required for the macro language's own special characters, among them APL's `+` and `*` — is read by the **preparser** as an opening quote wanting a closing `'`, and the api is rejected | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | — (`Syntax.rats:406-410` defines the escape; `PreCompilation.rats:115` puts the backtick in `Opening`; `PreParserState.java:256` pairs it only with `'` or `’`) | `apl/v1-probes/p08a_g.fsi` + `p08a_pre.out` (rejected), `apl/v1-probes/p08b_g.fsi` + `p08b_pre.out` (accepted), `apl/v1-probes/p08d_g.fsi` + `p08d_pre.out` (escape inside the expander brackets) | apl rung 1 | `Unmatched delimiter "api".` / ``Unmatched delimiters "`" and "end".`` The file is accepted only when an APL glyph appears **above** the escape: the preparser cannot tokenise such a glyph, its token loop (`Program = (Token w)+`, `PreCompilation.rats:55`, with no end-of-file anchor) then stops, `report()` never runs, and the delimiter check is silently skipped from there on. `apl/base/AplSyntax.fsi` keeps `⌿`, `⍬` and `⍎` above its escaped `+` for exactly this reason; `p08c_aplsyntax_pre.out` is its `Ok`. Inside the expander brackets the same escape also breaks the pairing of `⦇` with `⦈`: `Unmatched delimiters "(./" and "end".` Verified in this merge on the **retired first library**, from a git worktree of `0ff5eae27~1` (the retired library is that commit's `explorations/apl/v1/base`, its probes that commit's `v1/rung-1`, `v1/rung-2`, `v1/rung-2b`, kept here as `apl/v1-probes/`): re-run output as committed. |

| 286 | a **template that writes the host's caret drops its right operand silently**: `<[ (1.0 (l)) ^ (r) ]>` expands to `1.0 (l)` with no diagnostic, so `apl⦇ 10*10 ⦈` was `10.0`, `apl⦇ 2*3 ⦈` was `2.0`, and the causal mask came out `-10` instead of `-1E10`, which moved every loss from the seventh digit (step 1 `3.3659665281147144` against the golden `3.365966947584851`); the same rule through a one-line glue function `pow(b: RR64, e: ZZ32): RR64 = b^e` is exact | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap (the template translator; `^` is also the type-application and postfix-operator character, ledger rows 133, 144, 158) | — | `apl/mg/checks/model_run.out.1` (the wrong losses), `apl/mg/checks/model_run.out` (exact, through `pow`), `apl/mg/AplMg.fss` (`pow`), `apl/mg/AplMgSyntax.fsi` (the `*` rule) | apl rung 4b | Nothing in the ladder had put a host infix operator other than `+ - × ÷` inside a template, so nothing had caught it. Rule for a sub-language: no host caret in a template; call a function |
| 287 | **rule order decides only inside an ordinary nonterminal**: two alternatives of one macro bracket name backtrack over the closing bracket, so a shorter alternative placed above a longer one still lets the longer match; inside a nonterminal PEG commits to the first alternative that succeeds (ledger row 60's greed), so the folded reduction `+/l×r` must stand above the plain `+/v` there | POSITIVE-VERIFIED (the bracket), NEGATIVE-VERIFIED (the nonterminal) | design limit (PEG) | — | `apl/probes-4b/y06_fold.fss` + `.out`, `.out.0` (the four false diagnoses of the grammar before ledger row 65's bare-bracket-as-argument fact was recognised as the cause) | apl rung 4b | Row 65 met again: a macro bracket used bare as a juxtaposed argument does not match its rule; parenthesised it does |
| 290 | **an expansion arrives unparenthesized unless the template text itself writes the parentheses**, and the compiler's type checker requires a function's argument to be parenthesized, so `println(m⦇ … ⦈)` is `Argument to function must be parenthesized.` for every template that does not wrap itself; parenthesizing the macro bracket at the **use site** does not help, and outside argument position there is no problem | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap (the template translator loses `isParenthesized`) | — | `perf-probes/grammar-compile/shim/MatrixC.fsi` with `m01`-`m07`; `13-shim2-matrix.out`, `14-shim2-position.out` | grammar-compile probe | `<[ 42 ]>`, `<[ (a) ]>` and `<[ (a) + (a) ]>` all fail; `<[ ((a) + (a)) ]>` compiles and prints `42`; the same unwrapped template bound to a variable (`x: ZZ32 = m1⦇ ⦈`) compiles and prints `42`; `println((m1⦇ ⦈))` fails. Raised at `scala_src/typechecker/impls/Operators.scala:237`, reported against the grammar api's line 1 (row 243's placeholder span). Invisible to the interpreter, which never type-checks (`walk` leaves `fortress.compile.typecheck` false). Rule for a sub-language that wants to compile: **every template wraps itself in parentheses**. Reachable only behind row 288's shims. |
| 291 | **the type checker has no rules for template ASTs**, so the two mechanisms the APL base is built on do not survive `fortress compile`: a template naming a function declared in the using component (row 270) crashes the post-typecheck assertion with `ClassCastException: scala.runtime.BoxedUnit cannot be cast to AbstractNode`, and a typed lambda written inside a template (row 285) is `Unbound type: ZZ32` ×2, because a type written inside a template is never disambiguated | NEGATIVE-VERIFIED | implementation gap | — | `perf-probes/grammar-compile/shim/G_dblp.fsi` / `G_lamp.fsi` / `G_app.fsi` + `u_*.fss`, `16-shim2-rules-split.out`; the same three with the vocabulary imported into the grammar api (`VocabC`, `G_dblq`/`lamq`/`appq`), `17-shim2-vocab-in-api.out` | grammar-compile probe | Crash at `STypesUtil.assertAfterTypeChecking` (`STypesUtil.scala:1938-1948`) ← `StaticChecker.checkCompilationUnit:264` ← `checkApis:126` ← `TypeCheckPhase.execute:43`: the assertion hunts `OutAfterTypeChecking` nodes (`Juxt`, `MathPrimary`, `AmbiguousMultifixOpExpr`, `_InferenceVarType`) and a `<[ … ]>` body still holds them, whereupon the generated `Walker` meets a `BoxedUnit` and dies instead of reporting. `Unbound type` is `TypeWellFormedChecker.scala:95`, a bare `SVarType` the disambiguator never entered. No `TemplateGap` or `_SyntaxTransformation` case exists anywhere under `scala_src/typechecker/`. Moving the vocabulary into an api the grammar api itself imports changes nothing, so no import fixes it. Both mechanisms run on the interpreter (`02b-interpreter-usefn.out`: `42` `15` `103`). Consequence: rows 270, 282 and 285 — the APL ladder's whole division of labour — are interpreter-only facts. The plan for the four edits this would take is `reviews/template-checking-plan.md`. |

## Contested / unsettled

| # | claim | status | class | reproducer | found by | why unsettled |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 83 | `nat`-generic functions mis-unify on a second instantiation when called through an exported API | CONTESTED | implementation gap (claimed) | none saved — blinded's evidence is a transcript timestamp (`17:29`, the `src/MicroGPT.fsi` attempt) | blinded | No minimal reproducer survives, and building an API + component pair here did not isolate it. Kept so it is not re-derived from scratch; needs a fresh `.fsi`/`.fss` pair before it can be classified. run-b2's `apix` probes (rows 132, 133) build an API + component pair whose operators are functional methods and do not reproduce it — but they use no `nat` parameters, so the claim is still untested rather than falsified. Run C's `run-c/probes/api/` pair (row 151) does use `nat` generics — behind runtime-sized API signatures — at two different sizes, and does not reproduce it either; what remains untested is a `nat` static parameter appearing in the API's own signatures. |

## Disagreements resolved

1. **Chained subscripting / indexed power** — ours classified `m[i][j] → (m[i])[j]` as a *design limit* ("grammatical by design in the spec"); Astra and the blinded run called it an *interpreter gap*. **Astra and blinded are right** (rows 1-3): `concrete-syntax.tex:944-950` is left-recursive on `Primary`, `precedence.tex:44-51` puts subscripting and superscripting in one left-associative group, and `Expression.rats` parses both. The failure is `Evaluator.java:1251-1252`. Row 2 adds that it can also be silently wrong, which no source had.
2. **`f()[i]`** — blinded called it a grammar/design limit, and the review's correction of row 1 risked sweeping it in. **Blinded is right** (row 4): `juxtameaning.tex:157-161` makes it a static error and the interpreter quotes that sentence. Rows 1-3 and row 4 are different verdicts about neighbouring syntax.
3. **"The library cannot call the user's `opr +`"** — our sum experiment stated it unconditionally. **Falsified as stated** (rows 30-32): it holds for top-level `opr` declarations, not for functional methods. The important half is that the fallback is *silent*.
4. **`do … end` as a comprehension body** — blinded listed it as unavailable. **Falsified** (row 10).
5. **Transpose** — Astra recorded `transpose(W)` as a word and a remaining departure; the blinded run avoided needing it. **Ours is right** (row 34): the spec's postfix `^T` is declarable and runs.
6. **Both juxtaposition directions** — Astra judged `v M` unavailable; ours reached it via `Rank1 excludes Rank2`; blinded reached it with two plain overloads on unrelated object types. **Blinded's route is the simpler correct one** (row 33); `excludes` is needed only when the carriers share a supertype.
7. **`extends Number`** — Astra and ours agree it is illegal per `traits.tex:228-235`; ours adds that the walk path never enforces it and that the library types then work in full (rows 22, 52). Not a disagreement, a merge: the row needs both halves or it misleads.
8. **`W_1` / italic `W₁`** — the page-template ledger said italic `W₁` is unreachable. **Sharpened** (rows 15, 66): `W_1` is a lexical error, but `w17` renders `w₁₇`; what is unreachable is a *capital-initial* italic subscripted name, because capital-initial names render as type names.
9. **Σ over a user type** — the earliest of our notes recorded "Σ is sealed, impossible". **Retired by all three runs independently** (rows 40-41): impossible by *addition*, routine by *replacement*.
10. **Matrix pasting** — run-b (and its independent worker) recorded that matrix pasting fails, `[ A ; B ]` included; run-b2 recorded that a rank-one paste always fails but a rank-two paste of square blocks works. **Both are half right** (rows 106, 107): a grid with a single row *or* a single column of matrix elements always fails the extent check — `g18` settles the stacked `[ A ; B ]` of square blocks that run-b never reached and its worker inferred — while a 2×2 grid of square blocks pastes correctly and a 2×2 grid of non-square blocks throws `ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException`. Two distinct defects, one in `IUOTuple`, one in `IndexedArrayWrapper`.
11. **Matrix unpasting** — run-b classified it as a design limit (the spec's own `\note`), run-b2 as an implementation gap. **run-b is right** (row 108): `basic/matrix-unpasting.tex:15` declares it not yet supported, so it is classified as row 26 is.
12. **Object-member ordering** — run-b called "field/getter/setter before methods" an implementation gap because "the spec's object grammar orders nothing"; run-b2 called it a design limit and cited the grammar. **run-b2 is right** (row 124): `basic/objects.tex:172-176` and `appendices/grammars/concrete-syntax.tex:268-285` order the two groups. The neighbouring rejection of a `property` after a method *is* an implementation gap (row 125), because the same grammar puts `PropertyDecl` in both groups.
13. **Equality of value objects** — run-b read `P(1.0) === P(2.0) : true` as "`===` compares only the dynamic type"; run-b2 found that it compares the fields the object *body* declares, and that a body with none leaves the list empty. **run-b2's reading is right** (row 113), and it is the sharper claim: adding one field declaration makes the comparison work, and the shipped `Just` has none (row 114).
14. **Overloads on unrelated library types** — run-b met the rejection on two `Array` instantiations, run-b2 on `List` next to `Range`, and both classified it as the spec's Meet Rule. Both are right about the rule (row 97) — but the interpreter enforces it only on a cold cache (row 98, new here). Any future disagreement of the form "that program runs for me" should be re-checked with the component's `interpreter_parsed_cache` entry deleted.
15. **`pi`** — run-b's table first said `pi` is not defined and retired that on its own worker's evidence. The ledger keeps only the sharpened form (row 94): absent from the default library, present in the optional `Constants` API as a `FloatLiteral`, and unavailable under its Unicode name.
16. **`do … end` as a comprehension body, re-checked** — item 4 above settled the blinded run's row 14 against ledger row 10; because that blinded table is still in the tree saying the opposite, it was re-run in this pass: `g11` prints `<\|1, 2, 3\|>`. **Row 10 stands.** The `\|\|` half of the same blinded row is row 9, which run-b then completed (item above and row 9's amended note).

| 39. `strToFloat` (`FortressLibrary.fss:4192-4197`, whose own comment says "Quick and dirty"): every character is treated as a digit, so a sign counts as -3, and `10^k` is `ZZ32` and overflows at the tenth digit. | 40. Make a `nat`-generic function a value: passed as an argument it has no `FType`, so it can neither be checked against a parameter type nor key the callee's instantiation cache, and fails with three different diagnostics | 156 (1; under no item before) | `run-c/probes/q02c2_plaingeneric`, `q02c5_plainarrow_generic`, `q02c4_genericgeneric`, `q02b_arrow` |
| 41. Overload resolution on an untyped lambda argument: two overloads differing only in the arrow type of a function parameter are resolved by a typed lambda and not by an untyped one, which silently takes the first declared — a silent wrong answer | 164 (1; under no item before) | `run-c3/probes/g158_untyped_overload/G158.fss` |

**The APL feasibility merge (2026-09-15).** The study that preceded the ladder,
`explorations/apl-probes/REPORT.md`, ends in a table of eighteen candidate rows
about the syntax-extension mechanism itself, written before any APL was climbed
and never merged; four APL rows (180, 185, 188, 197) already cited it as if it
had been. All eighteen are entered here as **266-283**, none folded: no existing
row carried any of these claims, because nothing else in this tree had asked
whether the mechanism runs at all. Thirteen went into section 16, and one each
into sections 1 (278, the operator-character set), 2 (283, `narrow`), 6 (281,
the APL library) and two into 4 (279, 280, the shared glyphs and the enclosers).

What the eighteen add under the rows already there is the mechanism's floor and
its three unimplemented corners: the mechanism runs (266) and the spec's own
`syntax … = Expr` form does not (267); a grammar lives only in an api (269) and
the shipped `transformer/` examples are written in a dead spelling the build
never runs (268); templates resolve free names at the use site (270), which is
what every desugaring in the ladder depends on; and the three corners are a
bound `?` gap (274), a `*`-bound gap used without `**` (273) and `case … of`
over anything but `Cons`/`Empty` (275). Rows 276-277 are why a sub-grammar may
carry APL's glyphs and row 278 is why the host may not. Rows 281 and 282 are the
two halves — library and grammar — that the ladder then built on for six rungs.

Where a later rung row sharpened a feasibility row, the feasibility row is
entered with the cross-reference rather than dropped: 270 → 204 and 243,
272 → 197 and 20, 274 → 180 and 197, 276 → 232 and 181, 278 → 185, 216, 233,
279 → 216 and 233, 280 → 216, 281 → 192 and 7, 282 → 179-265, 283 → 188.

**The probe merge (2026-09-16).** Five reports, 23 rows entered as **288-310**, two
claims folded into rows this merge itself entered, and no new section. Five of the 23
are cost measurements and went into section 15, "Program shape and cost", beside the
interpreter measurements already there (139, 154, 163, 245, 256); a section of its own
for performance would now hold ten rows and is worth deciding on, but creating one is a
change to the ledger's shape and was left for Pavol.

**The numbering.** `perf-probes/grammar-compile/REPORT.md` numbered its four candidates
288-291 and two review documents written the next day already cite those numbers
(`reviews/template-checking-plan.md`, on rows 288, 290 and 291), so they keep them. The
other four reports' candidates are numbered from **292** in the order vocabulary, boxing,
kernels, prelude; each report now carries a line at the top of its candidate section
giving the ledger's numbers, and `perf-probes/prelude/REPORT.md`, which had no candidate
section, gained the four rows this merge wrote from its findings. The full map is in
`gap-ledger-probes/probes-merge/MERGE.md`.

**What the five add.** `run-c4/probes/vocabulary/` answered a question about Run C4's
40-declaration array api — habit or limit — and its ten rows (292-301) are the library's
own shape seen from a program: rank 3 carries no algebraic trait (292), `×` is excluded
from the array traits by design and the ring route breaks `+` (293), a user carrier is
shed by every library operation that allocates (294), one declaration generic in the
element type by its bound serves every element type and rank while the spec's own
algebraic bound does not fit `RR64` (295), a user generic declaration beside an imported
one is not checked and dispatches by specificity (296), the rank-3 spelling is forced
exactly where a family dispatches on rank (297), and the library's own row view and a
diagonal-as-`Matrix` cost 5× and 13-54× their hand-written alternatives (298, 299). The
three performance probes are about the other execution path: `boxing/` measured that
boxing is 2 % of the compiled scalar loop's 156× gap (302), `kernels/` rewrote that loop
the way microGPT-shaped code writes it and got 9.7× (303), found that no microGPT-shaped
kernel compiles at all because the compiler path has no array **type** (305, wider than
row 72), that a local function is `Can't compile LetFn` (304), and that boxing does cost
6.3-6.5× on array-shaped code (306). `prelude/` asked why the compiler needs its own
library and answered that almost nothing in the interpreter's is about code generation:
the checker cannot do `nat` static parameters at all (307), the numeric tower raises 92
errors under rules `walk` never applied (308), `builtinPrimitive` is the only
codegen-specific thing in the file and is 4.7 % of it (309), and plain Fortress library
code otherwise passes (310). `grammar-compile/` asked whether `fortress compile` runs a
user grammar: not as shipped, because every grammar api's import graph reaches the
interpreter's prelude (288); behind probe-only shims it does, to real bytecode (289); and
behind those the type checker has no rules for template ASTs, so a template must
parenthesize itself (290) and the mechanisms rows 270 and 285 record do not survive the
compile path at all (291).

**Folded rather than entered**, both from `prelude/`, which had no candidate table of its
own: its §1 (compiling `FortressLibrary.fss` as an ordinary component gives 1417
disambiguation errors, 78 % of them the two preludes colliding — the report calls it an
artifact) into row 288, the same collision reached from the other direction; and the
first half of its §4 (`FlatArrays` in the compiler's world, the same 77 errors) into row
305.
